That value goes straight into a `CanonicalHashWriter` and out through `ApiSpecTable.cs:28` as `ContentSha256`, which `SharpProof.CompilerArtifact/CompilerManifestArtifact.cs:21,42` folds into the compiler manifest hash as `apiSpecContentSha256` and the worker surfaces as `Summary.Versions.ApiSpecContentSha256`. **The file is not incidental**: `eng/acceptance/contract.json` gives the trusted-computing-base component `apiSpecificationIdentity` exactly **one** path, and it is this file. **The names it reflects on are generator output.** The nine declaration records - `SpecVariableDeclaration` through `SpecLengthDeclaration` - are emitted into `SharpProof.Specs/DefaultApiSpecCatalog.generated.cs:126-160` by `scripts/Generate-ApiSpecCatalog.ps1`, where the type names exist as hard-coded fragments in PowerShell string literals at `:383-458` (`"new SpecVariableDeclaration($role, $ordinal, $type)"`, `"new SpecBooleanDeclaration($literal)"`, and so on). So renaming a record in a PowerShell template, or changing the `Declaration` suffix convention, changes every API-specification digest and therefore every compiler manifest hash. **Nothing would notice.** The only test of this digest, `SharpProof.Specs.Test/ApiSpecContentDigestTests.cs:11-35`, asserts relative properties only - that reordering and duplicating exception metadata names yields the same digest and that different content yields a different one - and it varies only `exceptionMetadataNames`, never reaching the term-tag path. No absolute digest value is pinned anywhere in the repository, so a rename produces a silently different identity that every existing assertion still satisfies. **The repository already states the principle this violates.** `BannedSymbols.txt:46-47` bans `ISymbol.ToDisplayString` under the heading "Display text is presentation only and must not become a semantic identity", and the meta-analyzer ships `SPMETA004`, "Semantic string controls behavior" - and `SharpProof.Specs` is one of the 17 projects that set `SharpProofUsesMetaAnalyzer`, so that analyzer runs on this file. A census sharpens it: **`GetType().Name` appears seven times in handwritten production code, and six are message text** - exception names in `AnalyzerConfiguration.cs:124`, `FinalCompilationCollector.cs:57`, `Worker.Launcher/Program.cs:106`, and three fuzz/oracle messages. This is the only one that becomes an identity. **The fix needs no new machinery.** Three lines below the reflective tag, `:99-114` already switches exhaustively over all nine declaration types to extract their payloads and children. An explicit tag string per arm - the same closed vocabulary the IR uses for its own kinds - lives inside a `switch` the method already writes, and makes the digest depend on a declared constant rather than on what a generator happened to call a class. | `SharpProof.Specs/ApiSpecContentDigest.cs:93-124` (tag at `:97`, switch at `:99-114`); `SharpProof.Specs/ApiSpecTable.cs:28`; `SharpProof.Specs/DefaultApiSpecCatalog.generated.cs:126-160`; `scripts/Generate-ApiSpecCatalog.ps1:383-458`; `SharpProof.CompilerArtifact/CompilerManifestArtifact.cs:21,42`; `SharpProof.Specs.Test/ApiSpecContentDigestTests.cs:11-35`; `eng/acceptance/contract.json` (`apiSpecificationIdentity`, one path); `BannedSymbols.txt:46-47`; the seven-use `GetType().Name` production census; related R3540, R3320
- **R3740**: R3740 | **Four hand-written "all values" masks guard generated flag enums; a test already derives two of them from the enum itself, and the other two - one of which generated code consumes - are left out of it.** `SharpProof.Effects/EffectContractValues.cs:14-19` declares three completeness constants as bit arithmetic with a hand-written width: `AllEffects = (EffectContractKind)((1L << 16) - 1)`, `AllCapabilities = (EffectContractCapabilityKind)((1 << 13) - 1)` and `AllIncompleteReasons = (EffectAnalysisIncompleteReason)((1 << 4) - 1)`. `SharpProof.Specs/ApiSpecTable.cs:5-17` declares a fourth in the opposite style - `DefinedEffects` as an explicit `|` of twelve named `SpecEffect` members. All four sit over **generated** enums (`SharpProof.Effects/EffectContractMappings.generated.cs:72-79,108-127`; `SharpProof.Specs/DefaultApiSpecCatalog.generated.cs:15-31`), and **all four are correct today** - I checked: `EffectContractKind` has 16 non-zero members, `EffectAnalysisIncompleteReason` has 4, and `DefinedEffects` omits nothing from `SpecEffect`'s twelve. **The finding is which of them is machine-checked.** `SharpProof.Effects.Test/EffectContractWireParityTests.cs:101-114`, `DecoderMasksCoverExactlyTheNeutralVocabulary`, asserts `AllEffects == Enum.GetValues<EffectContractKind>().Aggregate(None, (all, v) => all | v)` and the same for `AllCapabilities`. That is exactly the right check - the mask is proved equal to the enum it claims to cover. **`AllIncompleteReasons`, three lines below the two it guards in the same file, is not in that test**, and neither is `DefinedEffects`. **The uncovered one is the one generated code depends on.** `EffectContractMappings.generated.cs:268` emits `new(typeof(EffectAnalysisIncompleteReason), (long)EffectContractMetadata.AllIncompleteReasons, true, [])` - so the generator writes a row referencing a hand-written constant whose value must match an enum the same generator writes. Add a fifth incomplete reason and the generator emits both the new member and a row still advertising a four-bit vocabulary; nothing compares them. `SharpProof.Effects/EffectSummary.cs:92` consumes it too. **The two uncovered masks fail in opposite directions, which is worth stating because it changes how each would be noticed.** `(1 << 4) - 1` fails **open**: a fifth member leaves the mask at 15, so a constant meaning "all" quietly stops meaning all. `DefinedEffects`' explicit OR fails **closed**: a thirteenth `SpecEffect` is simply absent from the mask, so `ApiSpecTable.cs:297` throws "The effect facet contains undefined flags" for a flag that is in fact defined - safe, but the message points away from the cause. **The remedy is already written in this repository.** Extending `DecoderMasksCoverExactlyTheNeutralVocabulary` with a third `Enum.GetValues<T>().Aggregate` assertion covers `AllIncompleteReasons` in three lines, and the same three lines in a `SharpProof.Specs.Test` fixture cover `DefinedEffects`. The style split is worth resolving at the same time: two masks in two assemblies express one idea as bit arithmetic with a magic width and as an explicit member list, and only the arithmetic form can be silently wrong. | `SharpProof.Effects/EffectContractValues.cs:14-19`; `SharpProof.Specs/ApiSpecTable.cs:5-17,297`; `SharpProof.Effects.Test/EffectContractWireParityTests.cs:101-114`; `SharpProof.Effects/EffectContractMappings.generated.cs:72-79,108-127,268`; `SharpProof.Effects/EffectSummary.cs:92`; `SharpProof.Specs/DefaultApiSpecCatalog.generated.cs:15-31`; `SharpProof.CompilerCollector/CompilerArtifact/CompilerWireMappings.generated.cs:167,207`; related R3320, R3720
- **R3760**: R3760 | **The repository generates twelve enum-to-enum identity translations at its wire boundary and hand-writes two more where there is no boundary at all - including a private clone of an enum the same file already imports.** A census of every `X.Foo => Y.Bar` switch arm in handwritten and generated production code finds **32** enum-to-enum mappings, of which **15** are pure identity - every arm maps a member to the identically-named member of another type. **Twelve of the fifteen are generated**, all into `SharpProof.CompilerCollector/CompilerArtifact/CompilerWireMappings.generated.cs` by `scripts/Generate-CompilerArtifactModel.ps1`: `OutputKind`, `Platform`, `ReportDiagnostic`, `NullableContextOptions`, `MetadataImportOptions` and `OptimizationLevel` to their `Compiler*` counterparts (`:17,41,79,56,68,31`), `BoundContractKind`, `BoundContractEvidence` and `BoundContractVariableRole` to theirs (`:246,257,268`), and `EffectEvaluationContractKind`, `EffectEvaluationReason`, `EffectEvaluationOutcome` and `EffectEvaluationCertainty` to their `Worker*` counterparts (`:114,139,128,153`). Those are correct: a domain type and its wire representation must be able to move independently, and because the translation is emitted from `CompilerArtifactModel.schema.json` it cannot drift from either side. That is the pattern, and it accounts for most of the population. **One apparent identity is not one, and the row says so rather than counting it:** `SharpProof.Gates/Corpus/CorpusGate.cs:578-589` maps `AnalyzerSemanticOutcome` to `CorpusVerdict` but adds `_ when hasNoDiagnostics => CorpusVerdict.SilentUnknown`, so the target vocabulary is genuinely richer than the source. Correctly two enums. **Two are hand-written translations between vocabularies that did not need to differ.** **(1) The clearer one is inside a single assembly.** `SharpProof.Effects/SwitchExpressionFacts.cs:3-8` declares `internal enum SwitchExpressionSelection { Never, Maybe, Always }`, used 84 times in its own file and also by `ManagedAbstractFlow.cs` and `OperationCompletionEvaluator.cs`. `SharpProof.Effects/ExceptionHandlerReachability.cs:3349-3354` declares `private enum SwitchSelection { Never, Maybe, Always }` - the same three members - used only in that file, and `:1917-1924` translates one into the other arm for arm with `_ => throw new InvalidOperationException("Unknown switch-pattern selection.")`. **The file already references `SwitchExpressionSelection` three times**, so the private clone crosses no boundary, serializes nothing and versions nothing; it exists only to be translated into. It is also in the 3,361-line file that R0312, R0950, R0973 and R3620 each circle for a different reason. **(2) The second spans two generated authorities.** `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractApiSymbols.cs:114-120` maps `ContractApiClauseRole` to `BoundContractKind`. Both enums are generated, from different catalogs by different scripts: `SharpProof.Frontend/ContractApiMetadata.generated.cs:16-22` gives `{ None, Requires, Ensures, Assume }` from `ContractApi.catalog.json` via `Generate-ContractApiCatalog.ps1`, and `SharpProof.Contracts/BoundContractModel.generated.cs:11-16` gives `{ Requires, Ensures, Assume }` from `BoundContractModel.schema.json` via `Generate-BoundContractModel.ps1`. One clause vocabulary, two JSON authorities, two generators, two assemblies, reconciled by a hand-written switch whose `_ => null` silently absorbs anything unmatched - so adding a fourth clause kind to one catalog and not the other produces no error, just a clause that is never recognised. **The shape of the finding is the contrast**: where this repository crosses a real boundary it generates the translation and cannot drift; the two places it writes one by hand are the two where the vocabularies did not need to be separate. | `SharpProof.Effects/SwitchExpressionFacts.cs:3-8`; `SharpProof.Effects/ExceptionHandlerReachability.cs:1917-1924,3349-3354`; `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractApiSymbols.cs:114-120`; `SharpProof.Frontend/ContractApiMetadata.generated.cs:16-22`; `SharpProof.Contracts/BoundContractModel.generated.cs:11-16`; `SharpProof.CompilerCollector/CompilerArtifact/CompilerWireMappings.generated.cs` (12 generated identity mappings); `SharpProof.Gates/Corpus/CorpusGate.cs:578-589`; the 32-mapping / 15-identity census; related R3620, R3740, R2100
- **R3780**: R3780 | **`ContractCanonicalization` instantiates the frontend's heaviest visitor - a class with its own ratchet entry - to call one method three times, never configures it, and the configuration it does build is handed to a different instance of the same class.** `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractCanonicalization.cs:9` declares `private readonly RoslynOperationLowerer _types = new(factory);`. The field name concedes the point: it is used at exactly three sites - `:284`, `:295` and `:307` - and every one is `_types.GetTypeId(...)`. `RoslynOperationLowerer` is one of the 16 files in `eng/acceptance/algorithm-size-ratchets.json`, ceilinged at 3,184 file expression nodes and 166 decision points, and constructing one allocates a `LoweringVisitor`, three `Dictionary` instances and a `List` (`SharpProof.Frontend/RoslynOperationLowerer.cs:6-19`). **The method it is built for needs almost none of that.** `GetTypeId` (`:119-155`) touches `_factory`, the `TypeSpecializer` delegate property, and the static helpers `CompilerIdentityBridge` and `CSharpScalarSemantics`. It uses none of the other nine fields - `_isKnownPure`, `_allowCompilerConstants`, `_variables`, `_instances`, `_captures`, `_captureOrder`, `_visitor`, `_currentLoweringResults`, `_missingInstance`. **And the instance is never configured.** `ContractCanonicalization` never assigns `_types.TypeSpecializer` or `_types.CustomLowering`, so the specializer keeps its default `static type => type` and each of the three calls runs an identity transform before doing its work. That is coherent - the canonicalization path genuinely wants unspecialized types - but it is nowhere stated, and the cost of expressing it is a whole visitor. **The specializer this class manufactures goes somewhere else entirely.** `CreateTypeSpecializer` (`:11-15`) has exactly one consumer, `ContractBinder.cs:197-201`, which passes it into `ContractExpressionBinder`; and `ContractExpressionBinder.cs:26-30` constructs a **second** `RoslynOperationLowerer`, this one configured with `TypeSpecializer = specializeType ?? (static type => type)` and `CustomLowering = BindIntrinsic`. So `SharpProof.Contracts` builds two instances of the same heavy visitor: one per contract binding doing real lowering with the specializer, and one long-lived field answering three type questions with the specializer left off. The class that owns the specializer factory holds the instance that does not use it. **The `IrFactory` parameter confirms the shape**: `ContractCanonicalization`'s constructor takes `factory` and uses it for nothing except `new RoslynOperationLowerer(factory)` - it is not stored. **The reduction is a small extraction, not a refactor.** A type holding `(IrFactory, Func<ITypeSymbol?, ITypeSymbol?>)` with `GetTypeId` on it is roughly 35 lines and needs nothing else; both the lowerer and `ContractCanonicalization` would use it. That removes one instantiation of a ratcheted class, drops the canonicalization path's dependency on the lowering visitor, and makes the unspecialized-types choice explicit rather than implicit in an unassigned property. `IsSupportedValueDomain` (`:157-158`) carries the same `(type, typeAlreadySpecialized)` shape and would move with it. | `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractCanonicalization.cs:3-15,284,295,307`; `SharpProof.Frontend/RoslynOperationLowerer.cs:5-19,119-158`; `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractExpressionBinder.cs:18-31`; `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractBinder.cs:197-201`; `eng/acceptance/algorithm-size-ratchets.json` (`RoslynOperationLowerer.cs` ceilings); related R2499, R2355, R0950
- **R3800**: R3800 | **A ninth site bypasses the framework-type-name policy R0482 documents, in a decomposed form that R0482's census could not have found: the BCL type name is split into four namespace segment comparisons instead of one literal.** R0482 records that `SharpProof.Specs/FrameworkTypeMetadataNames` states its own policy - "Keep framework type-name declarations in the spec layer so consumers do not grow independent string-based BCL authorities" - and finds **eight** production sites holding bare `System.*` type-name literals that bypass it. `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractForSymbolMatcher.cs:608-620` is a ninth, and it contains no `System.*` literal at all: `IsCompilerInAttribute` walks `type.ContainingNamespace` up three levels and compares `type.MetadataName` to `"InAttribute"`, then `interopServices.Name` to `"InteropServices"`, `runtime.Name` to `"Runtime"` and `system.Name` to `"System"`, finishing with `system.ContainingNamespace.IsGlobalNamespace`. It is `System.Runtime.InteropServices.InAttribute` spelled in four pieces - the same independent string-based BCL authority the policy forbids, in the one form a search for qualified literals cannot see. **The class already has the shape for it**: four of its 26 constants are attributes - `ConditionalAttribute`, `GeneratedCodeAttribute`, `ModuleInitializerAttribute` and `ReferenceAssemblyAttribute` - and `InAttribute` is simply absent. **It belongs in R0482's second category, not its first.** R0482 splits its eight by reachability; this one cannot reach the constants either. `SharpProof.Contracts.csproj:12-13` references only `SharpProof.Frontend` and `SharpProof.Ir`, and `SharpProof.Frontend.csproj:14-16` references only `SharpProof.Attributes` and `SharpProof.Ir` - there is no path from `SharpProof.Contracts` to `SharpProof.Specs`, and no file in `SharpProof.Contracts` mentions `FrameworkTypeMetadataNames` today. So the remedy is the one R0482 already identifies for the five `SharpProof.Meta.Analyzers` sites - a new project reference or the `Compile Include ... Link` mechanism - rather than a one-line swap. **A second reason not to reach for the obvious fix:** `IsCompilerInAttribute` is called from `RemoveCompilerInAttribute`, which is called from `ParameterRefCustomModifiersMatch(IParameterSymbol, IParameterSymbol)` - no `Compilation` is in scope anywhere on that path, so replacing the string walk with `Compilation.GetTypeByMetadataName` plus `SymbolEqualityComparer` means threading a compilation through three frames. Centralizing the *name* is the cheap half; symbol identity is a separate decision. **What makes this an outlier rather than a convention:** it is the only production site that identifies one specific BCL type by walking namespaces. The only other namespace-string comparison, `SharpProof.Meta.Analyzers/SharpProofSoundnessAnalyzer.cs:868-895`, routes through a named helper - `IsExactNamespace(type.ContainingNamespace, "System", "Collections", "Immutable")` and the same for `Frozen` - and matches whole namespace *families*, which no type-name constant could express. That use is justified and readable; this one is neither. | `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractForSymbolMatcher.cs:572-590,598-620`; `SharpProof.Specs/FrameworkTypeMetadataNames.cs:2-6,13,21,37,43`; `SharpProof.Contracts/SharpProof.Contracts.csproj:12-13`; `SharpProof.Frontend/SharpProof.Frontend.csproj:14-16`; `SharpProof.Meta.Analyzers/SharpProofSoundnessAnalyzer.cs:868-895`; extends R0482, related R0306
- **R3820**: R3820 | **The soundness analyzer opts into analyzing generated code and then exempts three generated types from its own rules by bare type name - names that originate as strings in two JSON data files, so renaming one in a schema silently deletes a soundness exemption.** `SharpProof.Meta.Analyzers/SharpProofSoundnessAnalyzer.cs:97-99` calls `ConfigureGeneratedCodeAnalysis(GeneratedCodeAnalysisFlags.Analyze | ReportDiagnostics)` - a deliberate choice to run on generator output. Three allowlists then carve generated types back out: `:664` returns early from the whole field rule when `field.ContainingType?.Name == "OperationSupportCatalogData"`, and `:669` and `:703` skip `StringFieldInIr` when the containing type's name `is not ("IrUnsupportedInfo" or "IrExceptionInfo")`. **All three are generated types, and their names live in data files.** `OperationSupportCatalogData` is emitted into `SharpProof.Frontend/OperationSupportCatalog.generated.cs:10` by `scripts/Generate-OperationSupportCatalog.ps1` from `SharpProof.Frontend/OperationSupport.catalog.json`; `IrUnsupportedInfo` and `IrExceptionInfo` are emitted into `SharpProof.Ir/IrModel.generated.cs:122,135` by `scripts/Generate-IrModel.ps1` from `SharpProof.Ir/IrModel.schema.json`, where they appear literally as `"name": "IrUnsupportedInfo"` at `:93` and `"name": "IrExceptionInfo"` at `:115`. So the chain is **JSON schema to PowerShell generator to generated C# to a bare `.Name` comparison in an analyzer in a different assembly**, and nothing along it declares the dependency. Rename a type in the schema and the exemption evaporates; the rule then fires on generated code, reporting a diagnostic against a file whose own header says "Do not edit this file directly". **The exemption is also invisible where it applies.** Nothing in the three generated files - or in the schema and catalog that produce them - indicates that these type names are load-bearing for a soundness rule. And because the comparison is on unqualified `.Name`, any same-named type introduced anywhere in the 17 projects that set `SharpProofUsesMetaAnalyzer` inherits the exemption silently. **The same file shows the principled form, forty lines away.** `IsCompilationScopedWeakCache` (`:904-925`) exempts compilation-scoped caches by *property*, not by name: it requires the type to be exactly `System.Runtime.CompilerServices.ConditionalWeakTable` via `IsExactNamedType(type, "ConditionalWeakTable", "System", "Runtime", "CompilerServices")`, requires two type arguments, and walks the first argument's base chain to `Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.Compilation`. That encodes a real justification - a weak cache keyed by `Compilation` is compilation-scoped rather than global mutable state - and it is why the repository's sixteen `ConditionalWeakTable` caches pass `SPMETA002` legitimately. **Four other name comparisons in the file are not part of this finding**, and the row says so: `:201` `"GetSemanticModel"`, `:318` `"Equals"`, and `:1073,1076` `"request"`/`"cancellationToken"` match *API shape* on symbols already resolved through `KnownSymbols`, which is how a signature must be identified. Only the three allowlists key behaviour to a name with no structural check. **The irony is load-bearing, not rhetorical**: this analyzer ships `SPMETA004`, "Semantic string controls behavior", whose description is that closed enums and typed records must represent semantic reasons - and its own exemptions are three string literals. It is the same family as R3720, where API-specification identity depends on generated type-name spelling, and R3800, where a BCL type is identified by decomposed name segments. | `SharpProof.Meta.Analyzers/SharpProofSoundnessAnalyzer.cs:97-99,661-687,703,904-925`; `SharpProof.Ir/IrModel.schema.json:93,115`; `SharpProof.Ir/IrModel.generated.cs:122,135`; `SharpProof.Frontend/OperationSupportCatalog.generated.cs:1-10`; `SharpProof.Frontend/OperationSupport.catalog.json`; `scripts/Generate-IrModel.ps1`; `scripts/Generate-OperationSupportCatalog.ps1`; the seven-comparison census; related R3720, R3800, R0482
- **R3840**: R3840 | **`ContractBinder` carries two identically-typed caches and two twelve-line entry points that differ in exactly two tokens - and the obvious consolidation is wrong, because the two modes are not subsets of each other.** `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractBinder.cs:10-13` declares `_bindings` and `_requiresBindings`, both `ConcurrentDictionary<IMethodSymbol, ContractBindingResult>` constructed with `SymbolEqualityComparer.IncludeNullability` - same key type, same value type, same comparer. `:68-83` (`Bind`) and `:85-100` (`BindRequires`) are the same twelve lines: null-guard the target, then `implementationBody == null ? <cache>.GetOrAdd(target, v => BindUncached(v, requiresOnly: <flag>)) : BindCore(target, implementationBody, requiresOnly: <flag>, CancellationToken.None)`. The only differences are `_bindings` versus `_requiresBindings` and `false` versus `true`. A third overload, `internal BindRequires(IMethodSymbol, CancellationToken)` at `:102-114`, duplicates the cached path again, reaching `BindCore` directly instead of through `BindUncached`. **The flag is threaded through seven decision points**: `:187`, `:215` (`includeResult: !requiresOnly`), `:242`, `:278`, `:292` (`if (requiresOnly && occurrence.Kind != BoundContractKind.Requires) continue;`), `:332` and `:358`. **The trap, and the reason this row exists rather than a one-line note:** it looks as though a requires-only binding is a filter of a full binding - drop every clause whose `Kind` is not `Requires` - so a reader could reasonably propose deriving one cache from the other, or dropping `_requiresBindings` entirely. That would change behaviour. Under `requiresOnly`, `ValidateIntrinsics` (`:332`) accepts violations whose `EnclosingClauseKind` is not `Requires`, and `BindClosedAttributes` (`:358`) skips a branch outright. So a method whose `Ensures` clause contains an intrinsic violation **fails** a full bind and **succeeds** a requires-only bind. The two results are not orderable, and the second cache is not storing a subset of the first - it is storing the answer to a different question. **What is actually duplicated is plumbing, not computation**: two cache fields that a single `ConcurrentDictionary<(IMethodSymbol, bool), ContractBindingResult>` - or a two-element array indexed by the flag - would replace, and three entry points over one core where the cancellation-token overload is the only one carrying information the others lack. **A detector note worth recording**, because it explains why the repository-wide structural-duplicate sweep in R3580 did not surface this: that lens normalises identifiers to placeholders but keeps `true` and `false` as keywords, so two bodies differing only in a boolean literal hash differently and are never grouped. Any future sweep for near-duplicates should fold boolean and small integer literals as well. | `SharpProof.Contracts/ContractBinder.cs:10-13,68-83,85-100,102-114,122-131,187,215,242,278,292,332,358`; related R0989, R2362, R2363, R3580

Preserve `NormalizeMethod`, `SymbolEqualityComparer.Default`, recording before the observer call, and observer invocation for every event, including repeated methods. `SharpProof.Analyzer.Test/RecordingSessionFactory.cs:34-46` separately counts events and combines observable outcomes; do not change that behavior or remove the shared `AnalyzerSemanticOutcomes.Combine`. Suggested validation: containerized Analyzer.Test outcome/reconciliation tests. Static evidence verified at HEAD 16b8e787c; no code changes or tests executed.

- **R3860**: R3860 | **"Decode UTF-8 text that may carry a byte-order mark" is implemented four times in production under three mutually incompatible policies, and the two implementations that read the *same file* disagree about whether it is valid.** The compiler manifest is written by `SharpProof.CompilerCollector/FinalCompilationCollector.cs:42` through `AtomicFile.WriteUtf8` (`SharpProof.Ir/AtomicFile.cs:4,70-76`), whose `Utf8 = new(false)` never emits a preamble. It is then read by two consumers that both call `CompilerManifestArtifactFile.ReadAllBytes` (`SharpProof.CompilerArtifact/CompilerManifestArtifact.cs:1014-1027`, which returns raw bytes and does no preamble handling) and both hand the result to `CompilerManifestArtifactJson.Deserialize` - **but decode it differently**. `SharpProof.Worker.Launcher/Program.cs:1108` calls `new UTF8Encoding(false, true).GetString(bytes)` with no preamble handling, so a BOM survives as a leading U+FEFF and the JSON parse throws; `SharpProof.Worker/WorkerInputSnapshot.cs:7,42,57-61` strips exactly one BOM first (`Utf8Preamble => [0xEF, 0xBB, 0xBF]`, `bytes.AsSpan().StartsWith(...) ? 3 : 0`) and accepts it. Two more policies exist elsewhere: `SharpProof.Gates/Corpus/CorpusSnapshotFormat.cs:31,51` **rejects** a preamble through a hand-written byte triple (`bytes[0] == 0xEF && bytes[1] == 0xBB && bytes[2] == 0xBF`), and `SharpProof.Worker.Protocol/ProtocolJson.cs:40,65` strips an **unbounded** number of leading marks at character level (`.TrimStart('\uFEFF')`). Reject, skip-one, strip-many, and don't-handle - for one concept, with no shared helper. **Checked negative: the divergence does not reach a digest.** `WorkerInputSnapshot.cs:31,51` hashes `manifestBytes` - the raw bytes *including* any preamble - through `WorkerProtocolJson.ComputeSha256` and `CompilerArtifactInputHash.Compute`, and `Program.cs:1109` likewise hashes `bytes`, so cache identity covers the preamble whichever decoder ran; this is a consistency finding, not a soundness one. **The consequence is that the worker's tolerance is dead.** On the launcher-driven path the launcher reads and rejects a BOM-prefixed manifest at `:1108` before the worker re-reads the same path, and no in-repo producer emits a preamble at all - so `Utf8Preamble` and its offset arithmetic have no reachable effect today beyond making two readers of one artifact disagree about what a valid manifest is. **Reachability splits the 12 production `UTF8Encoding` constructions into a choice and a constraint.** `SharpProof.Worker` and `SharpProof.Worker.Launcher` both project-reference `SharpProof.Ir` and `SharpProof.Worker.Protocol`, and `SharpProof.Gates` reaches both transitively through them, so those **8** sites could share one decoder - a choice. `SharpProof.Host` declares **zero** `ProjectReference` (`SharpProof.Host/SharpProof.Host.csproj`), so its 4 sites (`LinuxPathIdentity.cs:484,555,613,656`) genuinely cannot reach a shared helper; **this vindicates R0887's file-local scope as a constraint rather than an oversight, and R3860 does not restate it.** Two further symptoms of the missing helper: **five named statics for one encoder under four names** - `AtomicFile.cs:4` and `CorpusFileTransaction.cs:13` both `Utf8`, `PerformanceGate.cs:67-68` and `WorkerPerformanceProbe.cs:530-531` both `Utf8WithoutBom`, `ProtocolJson.cs:12` `s_strictUtf8` - where the two spelled `Utf8` are the only **lenient** ones (`new(false)`, substituting U+FFFD) and their names give no hint that they do not throw, even though `AtomicFile.WriteUtf8`/`WriteUtf8Async` are the canonical writers for manifests, responses and the verification cache; and **`WorkerInputSnapshot.cs:7` plus `CorpusSnapshotFormat.cs:31` both hand-roll the preamble that `Encoding.UTF8.Preamble` already exposes**, both assemblies targeting net9.0 where that property exists. A shared helper hosted in `SharpProof.Ir` or `SharpProof.Worker.Protocol` (both netstandard2.0) would need `GetPreamble()` rather than the span property. | `SharpProof.Worker/WorkerInputSnapshot.cs:7,31,42,51,57-61`; `SharpProof.Worker.Launcher/Program.cs:1108-1109`; `SharpProof.Gates/Corpus/CorpusSnapshotFormat.cs:31,51`; `SharpProof.Worker.Protocol/ProtocolJson.cs:12,40,65`; `SharpProof.Ir/AtomicFile.cs:4,70-76,84-88`; `SharpProof.Gates/Corpus/CorpusFileTransaction.cs:13`; `SharpProof.Gates/Performance/PerformanceGate.cs:67-68`; `SharpProof.Gates/Performance/WorkerPerformanceProbe.cs:530-531`; `SharpProof.CompilerArtifact/CompilerManifestArtifact.cs:1014-1027`; `SharpProof.CompilerCollector/FinalCompilationCollector.cs:42`; `SharpProof.Host/SharpProof.Host.csproj`
- **R3880**: R3880 | **"Does this claim carry effect evidence?" is answered by a linear scan on the per-claim loop path and by a hashed lookup everywhere else - and the hashed form already exists in the same file.** `CallableClaimResultAssembler.FromOutcome` (`SharpProof.Worker/CallableClaimResultAssembler.cs:13-15`) and `CallableClaimResultAssembler.Unknown` (`:130`) both evaluate `target.EffectClaims.Any(evidence => evidence.ClaimId == claimId)` for **one** claim id, scanning `ImmutableArray<CompilerEffectClaimArtifact> EffectClaims` (`SharpProof.CompilerArtifact/CompilerArtifactModel.generated.cs:102`) from the start every call. The same file already hoists exactly that lookup: `EffectClaimIds` (`:172-178`) builds an ordinal `HashSet<string>` once, and `Unknowns` (`:137`) and `PostconditionUnknowns` (`:157`) consume it. `SharpProof.Worker/CallableVerificationPolicy.cs:93-95` independently builds the same index a third way, as `target.EffectClaims.ToDictionary(evidence => evidence.ClaimId, StringComparer.Ordinal)`. **The scanning form is the one on the hot path.** `SharpProof.Worker/CallableVerifier.cs` calls the per-claim entry points from inside its per-postcondition loop at `:241`, `:247`, `:253`, `:276`, `:295` and `:317` - every `continue` branch and the main `FromOutcome` call - so a callable with *c* claims and *e* effect claims rescans the array once per claim, giving O(c x e) where the `EffectClaimIds`/`ToDictionary` form already in the codebase gives O(c + e). The three index constructions are also themselves redundant with one another: one `HashSet` of ids, one `Dictionary` of id to evidence, and two open-coded scans, for a single "claim id to effect evidence" relation on one `CompilerCallablePreparation`. Computing the index once per target and threading it through `FromOutcome`/`Unknown` - or caching it on `CompilerCallablePreparation` beside `EffectClaims` - collapses all four to one. **Distinct from R3848**, which covers proof-core re-normalization in the same two files and does not touch the effect-evidence lookup. | `SharpProof.Worker/CallableClaimResultAssembler.cs:13-15,130,137,157,172-178`; `SharpProof.Worker/CallableVerifier.cs:241,247,253,276,295,317`; `SharpProof.Worker/CallableVerificationPolicy.cs:93-95`; `SharpProof.CompilerArtifact/CompilerArtifactModel.generated.cs:102`
- **R3900**: R3900 | **A production overload of `EffectClaimResultAssembler.Assemble` has zero production callers and 15 test callers, and the two arguments it hardcodes are precisely the ones that select the branches those tests then cannot reach.** `SharpProof.Worker/EffectClaimResultAssembler.cs:5-14` is a two-argument convenience overload that forwards to the four-argument form with `CallableEntryFeasibility.Feasible` (`SharpProof.Worker/CallableEntryFeasibility.cs:12-17`) and `CancellationToken.None`. Counting every call site in the repository by top-level argument arity: the two-argument overload has **15** callers, **all** in `SharpProof.Worker.Test` (`CompilerManifestArtifactTests.cs:1178,1619,1915`; `EffectCounterexampleReplayTests.cs:23,98,147,206,271,306,327,409,485,530`; `ExceptionIdentityReplayTests.cs:209,388`), and **zero** in production. The four-argument form has 4 callers, of which the only two production ones are `SharpProof.Worker/CallableVerificationPolicy.cs:33-37`, which passes the genuinely computed `proof.EntryFeasibility`, and `:98-102` in `FailedLowering`, which passes `Feasible` explicitly because lowering failed and no feasibility was computed - so the constant is written at the one production site that means it, and the overload adds nothing production needs. **The hardcoding is not inert.** `Assemble` branches on `entryFeasibility.IsUnknown` (`:54-60`, returning `Unknown`/`Unavailable`) and `entryFeasibility.IsContradictory` (`:62-70`, returning `CallableClaimResultAssembler.Contradictory` with `VacuousEntry`), and observes cancellation at `:28`; pinning the parameter to `Feasible` and the token to `None` makes all three unreachable for the 15 callers that use it. Those branches are reached only by the two tests that call the four-argument form directly (`EffectCounterexampleReplayTests.cs:403,463`), so the convenience overload silently narrows what most of the effect-claim suite exercises. Deleting `:5-14` and having the 15 tests pass `CallableEntryFeasibility.Feasible, CancellationToken.None` explicitly removes a dead production member and makes the suppressed feasibility argument visible at each test that assumes it. | `SharpProof.Worker/EffectClaimResultAssembler.cs:5-14,28,54-60,62-70`; `SharpProof.Worker/CallableVerificationPolicy.cs:33-37,98-102`; `SharpProof.Worker/CallableEntryFeasibility.cs:12-17`; `SharpProof.Worker.Test/EffectCounterexampleReplayTests.cs:403,463`
- **R3901**: R3901 | **The single-project changed-test path creates and deletes a solution filter that it never reads.** `scripts/Invoke-SharpProofChangedTests.ps1:239-249` always allocates a GUID-named `.slnf` and serializes the selected project list before deciding at `:250` whether only one project was selected. For that case, restore uses the project directly (`:252-259`), build uses that same project (`:269-272`), and test execution either delegates to the architecture runner or invokes the resolved assembly with `vstest` (`:282-300`). Create the index lazily once per outer Decode invocation and pass it through private body decoding.
